The Frobenius condition, right properness, and uniform fibrations
Nicola Gambino, Christian Sattler

Abstract
. We develop further the theory of weak factorization systems and algebraic weak factorization systems. In particular, we give a method for constructing (algebraic) weak factorization systems whose right maps can be thought of as (uniform) fibrations and that satisfy the (functorial) Frobenius condition. As applications, we obtain a new proof that the Quillen model structure for Kan complexes is right proper, avoiding entirely the use of topological realization and minimal fibrations, and we solve an open problem in the study of Voevodsky’s simplicial model of type theory, proving a constructive version of the preservation of Kan fibrations by pushforward along Kan fibrations. Our results also subsume and extend work by Coquand and others on cubical sets.



Introduction

This paper studies the Frobenius condition for weak factorization systems (wfs’s), which asserts that pullback along a right map preserves left maps
[
3
,
13
]
.
One reason for the interest in this condition is that it is closely related to the right properness of a Quillen model structure, which requires that pullback along a fibration preserves weak equivalences.
Indeed, for a Quillen model structure in which the cofibrations are stable under pullback, such as the Cisinski model structures
[
9
]
, right properness is equivalent to the Frobenius condition for the wfs of trivial cofibrations and fibrations.
Furthermore, when pushforward along right maps exists,
i.e.
pullback along right maps has a right adjoint, the Frobenius condition is equivalent to the preservation of right maps by pushforward along a right map.
In the category of simplicial sets, this amounts to the preservation of Kan fibrations by pushforward along Kan fibrations, a fact that plays an important role in Voevodsky’s simplicial model of univalent foundations
[
29
]
.

For our development, we work in the setting of a category
\cal{E}
equipped with an appropriate cofibrantly generated wfs
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
, to be thought of as consisting of cofibrations and trivial fibrations, and a functorial cylinder satisfying appropriate assumptions.
Building on ideas in
[
9
]
, we then construct a new wfs
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
, to be thought of as consisting of trivial cofibrations and fibrations (
proposition

2.5
).
Here, a fibration is defined a map with the right lifting property with respect to a class of maps called here generating trivial cofibrations, which are obtained as (a generalized form of) pushout product between the endpoint inclusions of the functorial cylinder and generating cofibrations.
We then prove that the wfs
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
satisfies the Frobenius condition (
theorem

3.8
).
When applied in the category of simplicial sets, with the wfs of monomorphisms and trivial Kan fibrations
[
34
]
as
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
, fibrations in our sense are exactly Kan fibrations.
Therefore, our results yield a proof that the wfs of trivial cofibrations and Kan fibrations satisfies the Frobenius condition, and so give a new proof of right properness of the Quillen model structure for Kan complexes.
One reason for the interest in this proof is that it avoids entirely the use of topological realization, used in
[
22
]
, and of minimal fibrations, used in
[
27
]
.

We then obtain analogous results for algebraic weak factorization systems (awfs’s)
[
16
,
20
]
.
In an awfs, the left and right right maps do not just satisfy a lifting property, but are equipped with a natural family of solutions for lifting problems.
The technical ingredient underpinning this idea is to work not just with classes of maps in a fixed category
\cal{E}
, but with categories
\cal{I}
equipped with a functor
u\colon\cal{I}\to\cal{E}^{\to}
(often an inclusion or a forgerful functor), where
\cal{E}^{\to}
is the arrow category of
\cal{E}
.
For example, when
\cal{E}
is a presheaf category it is natural to define a uniform trivial cofibration to be a right map with respect to the category of monomorphisms and pullback squares, considered with the evident inclusion into
\cal{E}^{\to}
.
Unfolding the relevant definitions, a uniform trivial fibration is a map equipped with diagonal fillers for lifting problems against monomorphisms, with the fillers satisfying a naturality condition with respect to pullback squares between monomomorphism.
The presence of this algebraic structure remedies some unsatisfactory aspects of wfs’s (such as the failure of orthogonality classes to be closed under all colimits), something that has proved to be useful for applications (see
[
2
,
15
,
17
]
or
[
1
,
5
,
11
,
33
,
38
]
for examples).
The Frobenius condition has a natural counterpart in this context, called the functorial Frobenius condition
[
3
]
, which requires that the pullback along right maps not only preserves left maps but also preserves morphisms between them.

Starting from a suitable awfs
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
, whose left and right maps are to be thought of as uniform cofibrations and uniform trivial fibrations, we define uniform fibrations by algebraic orthogonality with respect to a category having generating trivial cofibrations as objects.
We then show that there exists an awfs
(\mathsf{C_{t}},\mathsf{F})
that has uniform fibrations as right maps (
theorem

6.5
) and that satisfies the functorial Frobenius condition (
theorem

7.8
).
Therefore, pushforward along a uniform fibration preserves uniform fibrations (
corollary

7.9
).
An important technical step in this development is the characterization of when an algebraically-free awfs (in the sense of
[
16
]
) has the functorial Frobenius property (
theorem

5.9
), which is more elaborate than its counterpart for ordinary wfs’s.

In order to apply our algebraic results in simplicial sets and cubical sets, we show that, given a category of monomorphisms and pullback squares satisfying some mild assumptions, it is possible to construct an awfs
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
satisfying the conditions necessary to apply our results (
theorem

8.1
).
Instantiating our definitions we obtain the notion of a uniform Kan fibration, which subsumes the one introduced in
[
11
]
.
We then establish the existence of an awfs that has uniform Kan fibrations as right maps and that satisfies the functorial Frobenius property, so that pushforward along a uniform Kan fibration preserves uniform Kan fibrations.

These results contribute to the ongoing investigations on whether Voevodsky’s simplicial model of univalent foundations
[
29
]
admits a constructive version,
i.e.
one that can be defined without the law of excluded middle or the axiom of choice.
In order to do this, it is necessary to overcome the problem that the the preservation of Kan fibrations by pushforward along Kan fibrations
[
29
, Lemma 2.3.1]
,
which is necessary for the interpretation of
\Pi
-types in the simplicial model, cannot be proved constructively
[
6
, Section 6]
.
Here, we solve this problem by working with the notion of a uniform Kan fibration, which is classically equivalent to the usual one, but allows us to prove constructively the preservation of uniform Kan fibrations by pushforward along uniform Kan fibrations.
Indeed, the proof of our results in the case of simplicial sets, as well as cubical sets, are constructive (see
remark

8.4
).

Furthermore, our results shed some light on the cubical set models of type theory that have been investigated extensively in recent years
[
1
,
5
,
11
,
33
,
38
]
.
Indeed, the independence results in
[
6
]
mentioned above have led the research community to shift the focus on models based on cubical sets and uniform Kan fibrations, since in that setting it is possible to develop a constructive model of univalent foundations
[
11
]
, showing in particular that pushforward along a uniform Kan fibration preserves uniform Kan fibrations.
But, as our results show, the key aspect that makes it possible to obtain that result on pushforward is the idea of using the algebraic notion of a uniform Kan fibration, rather than working with cubical sets.
Since our results apply to cubical sets, we give also a new proof of the fact that pushforward along a uniform Kan fibration preserves uniform Kan fibrations, avoiding entirely the complex calculations with cubical sets of
[
11
,
23
]
.
Indeed, one of the initial motivations for this work was to explore whether the theory of uniform fibrations in cubical sets could be developed at a greater level of generality, so as to make it applicable also to simplicial sets.

We also provide a characterization of uniform trivial fibrations in terms of the partial map classifer (
theorem

8.8
), as suggested to us by Thierry Coquand and André Joyal.
We then use this characterization to show in what sense the notion of a uniform (trivial) fibration subsumes the standard notion of a (trivial) fibration (
theorem

8.9
) In particular, we prove that a map in a presheaf category can be equipped with the structure of a uniform (trivial) fibration if and only if it is a (trivial) fibration.
As we observe in
remark

8.11
, however, the algebraic notion of a uniform fibration is essential for our results in
section

6
to hold constructively.

Remark

In order to make the paper more concise, we confine comments on constructivity issues to certain remarks and footnotes.
However, let us point out here for the interested readers that our development does not rely on the law of the excluded middle or the axiom of choice, apart from
propositions

2.5
,
6.5
and
8.1
.
For
theorems

6.5
and
8.1
, we argue that the result holds constructively for simplicial sets and cubical sets in
remark

8.4
.

Organization of the paper

section

1
introduces the setting in which we work throughout the paper.
The core of the paper is then organized in two parts: the first part (
sections

2
and
3
) studies wfs’s; the second part (
sections

4
,
5
,
6
and
7
) studies awfs’s.
In the first part, we introduce fibrations and establish the existence of the corresponding wfs in
section

2
, and then prove the Frobenius property for this wfs in
section

3
.
In the second part, we begin by establishing basic facts about orthogonality functors (
section

4
) and then establishing necessary and sufficient conditions for an algebraically-free awfs to satisfy the functorial Frobenius condition (
section

5
).
The rest of the second part then proceeds in parallel with the first part.

section

6
(parallel to
section

2
) introduces uniform fibrations and establishes the existence of the associated awfs, while
section

7
(parallel to
section

3
) proves the Frobenius property for uniform fibrations.
We end the paper in
section

8
studying uniform (trivial) fibrations in presheaf categories and relating (trivial) fibrations and uniform (trivial) fibrations.

1.
Background

Throughout the paper, we work with a category
\cal{E}
, assumed to be locally presentable and locally cartesian closed.
In particular,
\cal{E}
is cocomplete as well as finitely complete and pullback preserves all limits and colimits since it has both a left adjoint and a right adjoint.
For an arrow
f\colon X\to Y
, we write
f^{*}\colon\cal{E}_{/Y}\to\cal{E}_{/X}
for the pullback along
f
,
f_{!}\colon\cal{E}_{/X}\to\cal{E}_{/Y}
for its left adjoint, given by left composition with
f
, and
f_{*}\colon\cal{E}_{/X}\to\cal{E}_{/Y}
for its right adjoint, which we call pushforward along
f
.
We write
\cal{E}^{\to}
for the category of arrows of
\cal{E}
and
\cal{E}^{\to}_{\mathrm{cart}}
for the subcategory of
\cal{E}^{\to}
of arrows and cartesian squares.
Examples of such categories abound; in particular, our assumptions are satisfied not just by presheaf categories, but by arbitrary Grothendieck toposes.

Our definitions and results are illustrated in two running examples.
The first is the category
\mathbf{SSet}
of simplicial sets, defined as usual to be the category of presheaves over the simplex category
\Delta

[
19
]
.
The second is the category
\mathbf{CSet}
of cubical sets of
[
11
]
.
This is defined as the category of presheaves over the category
\Box
with objects of the form
\Box^{A}
with
A
a finite set and morphisms
\Box^{A}\to\Box^{B}
given by functions from
B
to the free de Morgan algebra on

A
.
Here, a de Morgan algebra is defined to be a bounded distributive lattice
(X,\land,\lor,\top,\bot)
further equipped with a unary operation
\neg(-)\colon X\to X
satisfying
\neg\neg x=x
, for all
x\in X
, and de Morgan’s laws:
\neg(x\land y)=\neg x\lor\neg y
,
\neg(x\lor y)=\neg x\land\neg y
, for all
x,y\in X
.

Thus,
\mathbf{CSet}
is the category of cubical sets with symmetries, diagonals, connections, and involutions.
Note that the presence of symmetries precludes
\Box
from being a Reedy category.
We stress that the only feature of the category
\Box
relevant for the main results are connections and symmetries.
Thus, our results apply equally to many other variations of cubical sets, excluding however those considered in
[
5
,
23
]
, which do not have connections.

Recall from
[
28
]
that a
functorial cylinder
in
\cal{E}
is an endofunctor
I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
equipped with natural transformations

\delta^{0}\otimes(-)\colon\operatorname{Id}{E}\to I\otimes(-)\,,\quad\delta^{1}\otimes(-)\colon\operatorname{Id}{E}\to I\otimes(-)\,,

called the left and right
endpoint inclusions
, respectively.
Such a functorial cylinder is said to have
contractions
if
\delta^{0}\otimes(-)
and
\delta^{1}\otimes(-)
have a common retraction
\varepsilon\otimes(-)\colon I\otimes(-)\to\operatorname{Id}{E}
, making the following diagrams commute:

connections
if, for
k\in\{0\,,1\}
, there is a natural transformations

such that the following diagrams commute:

(1.3)

0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.3126pt\raise-0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.3126pt\raise 0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.3126pt\raise-0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.3126pt\raise 0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.3126pt\raise-0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}{\hbox{\kern 71.25694pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I\otimes I\otimes(-)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.21518pt\raise-12.7998pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{c^{k}\otimes(-)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.21518pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 177.17346pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I\otimes(-)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 127.52194pt\raise 7.12001pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.37001pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{I\otimes\delta^{1-k}\otimes(-)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 123.17343pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.3126pt\raise-0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.3126pt\raise 0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.3126pt\raise-0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.3126pt\raise 0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.3126pt\raise-0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\hbox{\kern-0.3126pt\raise 0.94987pt\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 79.95828pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I\otimes(-)\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

We adopt the convention of associating the tensor product notation to the right.

If the functor
I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
has a right adjoint,

(1.4)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.6389pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.6389pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.99756pt\raise 10.80554pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{I\otimes(-)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.63895pt\raise 4.30554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.51392pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\bot}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 59.63895pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.9038pt\raise-10.80554pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\exp(I,-)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.6389pt\raise-4.30554pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

then we obtain a
functorial cocylinder

(\exp(I,-),\exp(\bar{\delta}^{0},-),\exp(\bar{\delta}^{1},-))\,,

i.e.
a functorial cylinder in the opposite category
\cal{E}^{\operatorname{op}}

[
28
]
.
Contractions and connections carry over as well.
In this case, note also that
I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
preserves colimits.
Sometimes we write
X^{I}
for
\exp(I,X)
.

Our main examples of functorial cylinders with contractions and connections, discussed below, arise from a monoidal structure, written
(\cal{E},\otimes,\top)
, and the presence of an
interval object
,
i.e.
an object
I\in\cal{E}
equipped with maps
\delta^{0},\delta^{1}\colon\top\to I
, called the left and right
endpoint inclusions
, respectively.
In such a situation, a functorial cylinder is given by tensoring with
I
.
It is immediate to isolate what structure on the interval object guarantees the presence of contractions and connections on the associated functorial cylinder: contractions are induced by a map
\varepsilon\colon I\to\top
and connections by maps
c^{k}\colon I\otimes I\to I
, for
k\in\{0\,,1\}
, provided that suitable axioms hold.
Note that if the unit
\top
of the monoidal structure is a terminal object, then we have contractions in a unique way.

Example 1.1
.

In
\mathbf{SSet}
, considered as a monoidal category with respect to its cartesian structure, an interval object is given by
\Delta^{1}
with endpoint inclusions
\delta^{k}\colon\{k\}\to\Delta^{1}
given by special cases of horn inclusions,
\delta^{k}=h^{1}_{k}
, for
k\in\{0\,,1\}
.
We have contractions since the monoidal structure is cartesian and connections
c^{k}\colon\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}\to\Delta^{1}
, with
k\in\{0\,,1\}
, given on points by
c^{0}(x,y)=\min(x,y)
and
c^{1}(x,y)=\max(x,y)
.
These are determined uniquely since
\Delta^{1}
and
\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{1}
are nerves of posets and
\min
and
\max
are monotone.

Example 1.2
.

The category
\Box
has finite products given by disjoint unions of sets, which makes cubical sets into a cartesian monoidal closed category.
In
\mathbf{CSet}
, an interval object with contractions is given by
\Box^{1}
with endpoint inclusions
\delta^{k}\colon\{k\}\to\Box^{1}
given by the maps from a singleton set to the free de Morgan algebra on an empty set that pick the bottom element for
k=0
and the top element for
k=1
.
We have contractions since the unit of the monoidal structure is a terminal object.
Connection operations
c^{k}\colon\Box^{1}\otimes\Box^{1}\cong\Box^{2}\to\Box^{1}
for
k\in\{0\,,1\}
are given by the maps from a singleton set to the free de Morgan algebra on a two-element set
\{x\,,y\}
that pick
x\wedge y
and
x\vee y
, respectively.

For the remainder of this section and the next one, we work with ordinary weak factorization systems (wfs’s)
[
8
]
and use standard notation and terminology.
For example, for a class
\cal{I}
of maps in
\cal{E}
, we write
{\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}}
for the class of maps having the right lifting property with respect to all elements of
\cal{I}
.
Let us recall the definition of the Frobenius property for a wfs.

Definition 1.3
.

We say that a wfs
(\cal{L},\cal{R})
in
\cal{E}
has the
Frobenius property
if the pullback of an
\cal{L}
-map along an

\cal{R}
-map is again an
\cal{L}
-map.

See
[
10
]
for an explanation of the connection between the Frobenius property for a wfs and Lawvere’s Frobenius condition
[
31
]
.
As mentioned in the introduction, a Quillen model structure
(\mathsf{Weq},\mathsf{Fib},\mathsf{Cof})
in which the cofibrations are stable under pullback (such as Cisinski model structures
[
9
]
, where the cofibrations are the monomorphisms) is right proper,
i.e.
the pullback of a weak equivalence along a fibration is again a weak equivalence, if and only if the wfs
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
of trivial cofibrations and fibrations has the Frobenius property.

We say that a wfs
(\cal{L},\cal{R})
is
free
on a class of maps
\cal{I}
(not necesssarily a set) if

\cal{R}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}}
.
If this is the case, then
\cal{L}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-74.20758pt\pitchfork\hskip 74.20758pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-74.20758pt\hskip 74.20758pt}}}{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-74.20758pt\pitchfork\hskip 74.20758pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-74.20758pt\hskip 74.20758pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.33624pt\hskip 5.33624pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-5.33624pt\hskip 5.33624pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.81158pt\hskip 3.81158pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.81158pt\hskip 3.81158pt}}}})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-59.81044pt\pitchfork\hskip 59.81044pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-59.81044pt\hskip 59.81044pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.81158pt\hskip 3.81158pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-3.81158pt\hskip 3.81158pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-2.72256pt\hskip 2.72256pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-2.72256pt\hskip 2.72256pt}}}})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-54.08119pt\pitchfork\hskip 54.08119pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-54.08119pt\hskip 54.08119pt}}}}
.
Of course, a wfs could be free on many different classes of maps.
By Quillen’s small object argument
[
34
]
, every set
\cal{I}
determines a wfs
(\cal{L},\cal{R})
free on
\cal{I}
.
These are what we call
cofibrantly generated wfs’s
.
The next, very simple, proposition provides a convenient way of checking that a cofibrantly generated wfs satisfies the Frobenius condition and will be used in the proof of
theorem

3.8
.

Proposition 1.4
.

Let
(\cal{L},\cal{R})
be a wfs that is free on a class of maps
\cal{I}
.
Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

(\cal{L},\cal{R})
has the Frobenius property.

(ii)

The pushforward along an
\cal{R}
-map preserves
\cal{R}
-maps.

(iii)

The pullback of an
\cal{I}
-map along an
\cal{R}
-map is an
\cal{L}
-map.

The claims follow by standard properties of the interaction between lifting properties and adjoint functors.
∎

As we will see in
section

5
, the algebraic counterpart of
proposition

1.4
, given by
theorem

5.9
, is rather more complex to prove.

2.
Fibrations

From here until the end of
section

3
we assume that our fixed category
\cal{E}
is equipped with a functorial cylinder
I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
with contractions and connections as well as a right adjoint as in (
1.4
).

The notion of a fibration that we introduce and study in this section and the next is relative not only to
\cal{E}
and its functorial cylinder, but also to a wfs on
\cal{E}
satisfying some assumptions that we encapsulate in the notion of a
suitable wfs
, given in
definition

2.1
below.
In order to state that definition, we need some notation.
For
k\in\{0,1\}
and a map
i\colon A\to B
in
\cal{E}
, we define the
Leibniz product

[
36
]
of the endpoint inclusion
\delta^{k}\otimes(-)
and
i
to be the map

(2.1)

\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i\colon(I\otimes A)+_{A}B\to I\otimes B

determined uniquely by the following pushout diagram:

(2.2)

A)+_{A}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.57587pt\raise-41.83679pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.61111pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 147.01897pt\raise-57.22916pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.05284pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 10.18231pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\line@@}}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\lower 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\line@@}}}\hbox{}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 159.7637pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-63.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 78.23515pt\raise-63.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 147.01897pt\raise-63.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I\otimes B\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

We shall use Leibniz products with endpoint inclusions throughout the paper.
In the next definition and afterwards, we write
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
to denote a suitable wfs, and refer to maps in
\mathsf{Cof}
as
cofibrations
and maps in
\mathsf{TrivFib}
as
trivial fibrations
since this helps us to convey some of the basic intuition motivating our development.

Definition 2.1
.

A wfs
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
in
\cal{E}
is said to be
suitable
if the following hold:

(S1)

(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
is cofibrantly generated.

(S2)

Every object of
\cal{E}
is cofibrant,
i.e.
for every
X\in\cal{E}
, the unique map
\bot_{X}\colon 0\to X
is a cofibration.

(S3)

Cofibrations are closed under pullback,
i.e.
for every pullback square

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{X\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

if
i\in\mathsf{Cof}
then
j\in\mathsf{Cof}
.

(S4)

Cofibrations are closed under Leibniz product with the endpoint inclusions,
i.e.
for every
i\in\mathsf{Cof}
we have
\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i\in\mathsf{Cof}
.

Example 2.2
.

Let
\cal{E}
be a presheaf category.
By a result of Cisinski
[
9
, Proposition 1.2.27]
,
\cal{E}
admits a cofibrantly generated wfs whose cofibrations are the mononomorphisms.
1
1

1

If
\cal{E}
is a category of presheaves over an elegant Reedy category
[
4
]
, it is possible to be more specific: the boundary inclusions (
i.e.
the latching objects inclusions for representables) form a set of generating cofibrations.

This wfs then satisfies conditions (S1) to (S3) of
definition

2.1
.
For it to be suitable,
i.e.
to also satisfy condition (S4), it suffices that the endpoint inclusions
\delta^{0}
and
\delta^{1}
are cartesian natural transformations.
This will be the case if the functorial cylinder is induced by cartesian product with an interval object as in our main examples.

Let us now fix a suitable wfs
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
.
We let
\cal{I}
be a set of maps that cofibrantly generates it, which exists by assumption (S1), and refer its elements as
generating cofibrations
.
We wish to define a new wfs, to be thought of as consisting of trivial cofibrations and fibrations.
For this, we begin by defining the set of maps that determines the notion of a fibration.
This set is defined by letting

(2.3)

\cal{I}_{\otimes}=_{\operatorname{def}}\{\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i\mid k\in\{0\,,1\}\,,\;i\in\cal{I}\}\,.

We refer to elements of
\cal{I}_{\otimes}
as
generating trivial cofibrations
.

Definition 2.3
.

A
fibration
is a map with the right lifting property with respect to all generating trivial cofibrations.

Before illustrating the notion of a fibration in our running examples, we establish that it is actually independent of the choice of the set of generating cofibrations and that fibrations are the right class of a wfs.
For the first goal, we use the cocylinder structure on the right adjoint
\exp(I,-)
to
I\otimes(-)
.
For
k\in\{0\,,1\}
and a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\cal{E}
, we define the
Leibniz exponential
of
\exp(\bar{\delta}^{k},-)
and
f
to be the map

\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta}^{k},f)\colon X^{I}\to Y^{I}\times_{Y}X

determined uniquely by the following pullback diagram:

(2.4)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.92181pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.82881pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.76834pt\raise 13.72537pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 107.7497pt\raise-49.3684pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 60.14471pt\raise-73.25009pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.67557pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\exp(\bar{\delta}^{k},Y)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.73581pt\raise-65.82452pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 100.73581pt\raise-65.82452pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

This definition can be extended so as to determine a functor, right adjoint to the Leibniz product functor:

(2.5)

\textstyle{\cal{E}^{\to}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}(-)}
\scriptstyle{\bot}
\textstyle{\cal{E}^{\to}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta}^{k},-)}

The desired characterization of fibrations, stated in the next proposition, now follows from standard adjointness arguments.

Proposition 2.4
.

For a map
p\colon X\to Y
in
\cal{E}
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

p
is a fibration,

(ii)

\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta^{0}},p)
and
\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta^{1}},p)
are trivial fibrations. ∎

We now define a
trivial cofibration
to be a map with the left lifting property with respect to all fibrations.
We write
\mathsf{Fib}
for the class of fibrations and
\mathsf{TrivCof}
for the class of trivial cofibrations, so that
\mathsf{Fib}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{(\cal{I}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.4181pt\hskip 25.4181pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.4181pt\hskip 25.4181pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{(\cal{I}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.4181pt\hskip 25.4181pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.4181pt\hskip 25.4181pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{(\cal{I}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-21.21776pt\hskip 21.21776pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-21.21776pt\hskip 21.21776pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{(\cal{I}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-19.52873pt\hskip 19.52873pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-19.52873pt\hskip 19.52873pt}}}}
and
\mathsf{TrivCof}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-18.43057pt\pitchfork\hskip 18.43057pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.43057pt\hskip 18.43057pt}}}{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-18.43057pt\pitchfork\hskip 18.43057pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.43057pt\hskip 18.43057pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-13.36324pt\pitchfork\hskip 13.36324pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.36324pt\hskip 13.36324pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-10.14238pt\pitchfork\hskip 10.14238pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.14238pt\hskip 10.14238pt}}}}
.
The next proposition is immediate.

Proposition 2.5
.

(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
is a wfs in
\cal{E}
.

By Quillen’s small object argument, with
\cal{I}_{\otimes}
as the generating set.
∎

Example 2.6
.

In
\mathbf{SSet}
, let
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
be the wfs of monomorphisms and trivial Kan fibrations, which is cofibrantly generated by the set of boundary inclusions

\cal{I}=\{i^{n}\colon\partial\Delta^{n}\to\Delta^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Then a fibration in our sense is a map with the right lifting property with respect to all maps of the form

h_{k}^{1}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i^{n}\colon(\Delta^{1}\times\partial\Delta^{n})\cup(\{k\}\times\Delta^{n})\to\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{n}\,,

given by the Leibniz product of an endpoint inclusion
h_{k}^{1}\colon\{k\}\to\Delta^{1}
with a boundary inclusion
i^{n}\colon\partial\Delta^{n}\to\Delta^{n}
.
This is shown equivalent to the usual notion of Kan fibration in
[
12
, Chap. IV, Sec. 2]
.
Thus, the wfs
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
of
proposition

2.5
is the wfs of trivial cofibrations and Kan fibrations associated to the Quillen model structure for Kan complexes
[
34
]
.

Example 2.7
.

In
\mathbf{CSet}
, let
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
be the wfs in which
\mathsf{Cof}
consists of all monomorphisms, which is suitable by
example

2.2
.
A fibration in
\mathbf{CSet}
will be called a
Kan fibration
.
To illustrate the relationship with the standard Kan filling condition for cubes, let
i^{n}\colon\partial\Box^{n}\to\Box^{n}
, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote the boundary inclusion of the
n
-cube
\Box^{n}
, which is given by the
n
-fold Leibniz product of
[\delta^{0},\delta^{1}]\colon\{0\,,1\}\to\Box^{1}
.
The Leibniz product in
\mathbf{CSet}
can be seen to preserve monomorphisms, hence
i^{n}
will be a cofibration.
Note however that the boundary inclusions
i^{n}
will not form a generating set of cofibrations:
for example, the inclusion
\Box^{1}\to\Box^{2}
given by the diagonal does not lie in the saturation of the boundary inclusions.
The maps

\delta^{0}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i^{n}\colon\sqcup_{1}^{1+n}\to\Box^{1+n}\,,\quad\delta^{1}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i^{n}\colon\sqcap_{1}^{1+n}\to\Box^{1+n}\,,

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, are trivial cofibrations.
Since the cube category has symmetries, a Kan fibration in
\mathbf{CSet}
has the right lifting property also with respect to open box inclusions, which are counterparts in cubical sets of the horn inclusions in simplicial sets.
However, these will not form a generating set of trivial cofibrations.

Remark 2.8
.

Let us also relate the notion of a fibration introduced here with the notion of a naive fibration introduced by Cisinski
[
9
]
.
So let
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
be a wfs as in
example

2.2
and let
\cal{I}
be a generating set of cofibrations.
Let
m\otimes(-)\colon\operatorname{Id}{E}+\operatorname{Id}{E}\to I\otimes(-)
be the natural transformation with components

[\delta^{0}\otimes X,\delta^{1}\otimes X]\colon X+X\to I\otimes X

for
X\in\cal{E}
.
A
naive Cisinski fibration
would be defined as a map with the right lifting property with respect to the class

\displaystyle\cal{I}_{\otimes}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=_{\operatorname{def}}\{(m\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}(-))^{n}(\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i)\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\,,\;k\in\{0\,,1\}\,,\;i\in\cal{I}\}

\displaystyle=_{\phantom{\operatorname{def}}}\{m\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\ldots\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}m\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i\mid k\in\{0\,,1\}\,,\;i\in\cal{I}\}.

Informally, one could think of this set as the closure of the set of generating trivial cofibrations
\cal{I}_{\otimes}
under Leibniz product with
m
.
Since
\cal{I}_{\otimes}\subseteq\cal{I}^{\prime}_{\otimes}
, every naive Cisinski fibration is a fibration in our sense.

Our reason for working with
\cal{I}_{\otimes}
instead of this class is that they coincide in a lot of cases.
For example, assume that the functorial cylinder is induced by tensoring with an interval object in a symmetric monoidal category and
\mathsf{Cof}
is closed under Leibniz product with the boundary inclusion
[\delta^{0},\delta^{1}]\colon 1+1\to I
, which is the case in our main examples, where
\mathsf{Cof}
consists of all monomorphisms, the map
[\delta^{0},\delta^{1}]
is a monomorphism, and Leibniz product preserves monomorphisms.
Then, by permuting the Leibniz product, one sees that every element of
\cal{I}_{\otimes}^{\prime}
is isomorphic to an element of
\cal{I}_{\otimes}
.

To discuss a second case where fibrations and naive Cisinski fibrations coincide, we use some notions and results from
section

3
.
If we assume generalized connections (in the sense of
(1+n+1)
-ary operations that behave as connections for fixed middle
n
arguments), and that
\mathsf{Cof}
is closed under Leibniz product with
[\delta^{0},\delta^{1}]
(for example, this is the case if
[\delta^{0},\delta^{1}]
is a monomorphism and the maps in
\mathsf{Cof}
are the monomorphisms in a presheaf category), then the maps in
\cal{I}^{\prime}_{\otimes}
can still be shown to be strong homotopy equivalences.
This gives rise to an inclusion
\cal{I}_{\otimes}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{Cof}\cap S
(where
S
is the class of strong homotopy equivalences that we define in
section

3
).
By
lemma

3.5
, it then follows that every fibration is a naive Cisinski fibration.

We conclude this section with some observations on the wfs’s
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
and
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
.
First, since
\mathsf{Cof}
is closed under Leibniz products with the endpoint inclusions, we have that
\cal{I}_{\otimes}\subseteq\mathsf{Cof}
.
Therefore, since
\mathsf{Cof}
is saturated, we have
\mathsf{TrivCof}\subseteq\mathsf{Cof}
,
i.e.
every trivial cofibration is a cofibration.
Second, observe that
proposition

2.4
implies, by standard orthogonality arguments, that
\mathsf{Fib}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{(\mathsf{Cof}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-34.15285pt\hskip 34.15285pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-34.15285pt\hskip 34.15285pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{(\mathsf{Cof}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-34.15285pt\hskip 34.15285pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-34.15285pt\hskip 34.15285pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{(\mathsf{Cof}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-26.3661pt\hskip 26.3661pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-26.3661pt\hskip 26.3661pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{(\mathsf{Cof}_{\otimes})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.07306pt\hskip 22.07306pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-22.07306pt\hskip 22.07306pt}}}}
, where
\mathsf{Cof}_{\otimes}=_{\operatorname{def}}\{\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}i\mid k\in\{0\,,1\}\,,\,i\in\mathsf{Cof}\,\}
.
Finally, we establish a corollary of
proposition

2.4
which will be used to prove
lemmas

3.5
and
3.7
, which are two of the key steps in establishing the Frobenius property for the wfs
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
in
section

3
.

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
in
\cal{E}
.
If
f\in\mathsf{Cof}
, then
\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\in\mathsf{TrivCof}
, for
k\in\{0\,,1\}
.

Assume
f\in\mathsf{Cof}
and let
k\in\{0\,,1\}
.
In order to show that
\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\in\mathsf{TrivCof}
, it suffices to show that
\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\in{\mathchoice{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.01396pt\hskip 36.01396pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.01396pt\hskip 36.01396pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.01396pt\hskip 36.01396pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.01396pt\hskip 36.01396pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.93407pt\hskip 18.93407pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.93407pt\hskip 18.93407pt}}}})}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-151.8143pt\pitchfork\hskip 151.8143pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-151.8143pt\hskip 151.8143pt}}}{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 36.01396pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.01396pt\hskip 36.01396pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-36.01396pt\hskip 36.01396pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 151.8143pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-151.8143pt\hskip 151.8143pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-25.67159pt\hskip 25.67159pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.72177pt\hskip 17.72177pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.72177pt\hskip 17.72177pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{({\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.93407pt\hskip 18.93407pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.93407pt\hskip 18.93407pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.93407pt\hskip 18.93407pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.93407pt\hskip 18.93407pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-12.64651pt\hskip 12.64651pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-12.64651pt\hskip 12.64651pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{TrivCof}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.04509pt\hskip 9.04509pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip ,
i.e.
\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\in{\mathchoice{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-18.43057pt\pitchfork\hskip 18.43057pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.43057pt\hskip 18.43057pt}}}{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-18.43057pt\pitchfork\hskip 18.43057pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.43057pt\hskip 18.43057pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-13.36324pt\pitchfork\hskip 13.36324pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.36324pt\hskip 13.36324pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-10.14238pt\pitchfork\hskip 10.14238pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.14238pt\hskip 10.14238pt}}}}
.
We have

\displaystyle\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\in{\mathchoice{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-18.43057pt\pitchfork\hskip 18.43057pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.43057pt\hskip 18.43057pt}}}{{\hskip 5.3889pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip-5.3889pt\hskip-18.43057pt\pitchfork\hskip 18.43057pt}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-18.43057pt\hskip 18.43057pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-13.36324pt\pitchfork\hskip 13.36324pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.36324pt\hskip 13.36324pt}}}{{\hskip 4.72229pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\mathsf{Fib}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip-4.72229pt\hskip-10.14238pt\pitchfork\hskip 10.14238pt}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.14238pt\hskip 10.14238pt}}}}

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\forall p\in\mathsf{Fib})\,\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\pitchfork p

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(\forall p\in\mathsf{Fib})\,f\pitchfork\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta}^{k},p)

But we know that if
p\in\mathsf{Fib}
, then
\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta}^{k},p)\in\mathsf{TrivFib}
by
proposition

2.4
.
∎

3.
The Frobenius property for fibrations

We continue to work with our fixed locally presentable and locally cartesian closed category
\cal{E}
, equipped with a functorial cylinder
I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
with contractions and connections, and with a right adjoint as in (
1.4
), and a fixed suitable wfs
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{TrivFib})
in
\cal{E}
, with generating set
\cal{I}
.

Our aim in this section is to show that the wfs
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
of
proposition

2.5
satisfies the Frobenius property.
Our proof of the Frobenius property consists of three main steps.
First, we introduce the notion of a
strong homotopy equivalence
(
definition

3.1
) and give a characterization of strong homotopy equivalences as certain retracts (
lemma

3.3
).
Second, we show that every generating trivial cofibration is both a cofibration and a strong homotopy equivalence and that the cofibrations which are strong homotopy equivalences are trivial cofibrations (
lemma

3.5
).
Third, we show that the pullback of a strong homotopy equivalence along a fibration is again a strong homotopy equivalence (
lemma

3.7
).
With these facts in place, the Frobenius condition for
(\mathsf{TrivCof},\mathsf{Fib})
follows easily (
theorem

3.8
).

We begin by introducing strong homotopy equivalences.
For this, we need to review some preliminary notions.
Let
f,g\colon X\to Y
be maps in
\cal{E}
.
Recall that a
homotopy from
f
to
g
, denoted
\phi\colon f\sim g
, is a morphism
\phi\colon I\otimes X\to Y
such that the following diagram commutes:

We say that a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\cal{E}
is called a
left
(or
0
-oriented
)
homotopy equivalence
if there exist
g\colon Y\to X
and homotopies
\phi\colon g\mathbin{\circ}f\sim\operatorname{id}_{X}
,
\psi\colon f\mathbin{\circ}g\sim\operatorname{id}_{Y}
.
Dually, we have a
right
(or

1
-oriented
)
homotopy equivalence
if there exist
g\colon Y\to X
and homotopies
\phi\colon\operatorname{id}_{X}\sim g\mathbin{\circ}f
,
\psi\colon\operatorname{id}_{Y}\sim f\mathbin{\circ}g
.
The notion of a strong homotopy equivalence, defined below, is obtained by requiring an additional condition on the homotopies
\phi
and
\psi
.

Definition 3.1
.

For
k\in\{0\,,1\}
, a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\cal{E}
is called a
strong
k
-oriented homotopy equivalence
if there is a map
g
and homotopies
\phi
and
\psi
making
f
into a
k
-oriented homotopy equivalence such that the following diagram commutes:

(3.2)

We write
S_{k}
for the class of strong
k
-oriented homotopy equivalences and let
S=_{\operatorname{def}}S_{0}\cup S_{1}
.
Elements of
S
are called
strong homotopy equivalences
.

Remark 3.2
.

The components of the endpoint inclusion
\delta^{k}\otimes(-)\colon\operatorname{Id}{E}\to I\otimes(-)
are strong
k
-oriented homotopy equivalences.
In fact, they are strong
k
-oriented deformation retracts,
i.e.
strong
k
-oriented homotopy equivalences for which the homotopy
\phi
is trivial,
i.e.
\phi=\varepsilon\otimes X
, where
\varepsilon
is the contraction of the functorial cylinder.
To show this, one exploits crucially the assumption that the functorial cylinder has connections.
For
k=0
, the retraction is given by
\varepsilon\otimes X\colon I\otimes X\to X
and the homotopy
\psi\colon(\delta^{0}\otimes X)\circ(\varepsilon\otimes X)\sim 1_{I\otimes X}
is given by the connection
c^{0}\otimes X
.
The correctness of the left and right endpoints of
\psi
follow from the left sides of (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), respectively.
The axiom for strength follows from the right part of (
1.2
).
The case
k=1
is similar.

Let us give an alternative characterization of strong
k
-oriented homotopy equivalences.

k\in\{0\,,1\}
, we have the diagram

0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I\otimes Y\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

which commutes by the naturality of
\delta^{1-k}\otimes(-)
since
(\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f)\mathbin{\circ}\iota_{0}=I\otimes f
.
This diagram gives us (naturally in
f
) a map
\theta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\colon f\to\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f
in
\cal{E}^{\to}
, which we use in the next lemma to provide a characterization of strong homotopy equivalences as retractions.

Lemma 3.3
.

A map
f\colon X\to Y
is a strong
k
-oriented homotopy equivalence if and only if the map
\theta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f\colon f\to\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f
exhibits
f
as a retract of
\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f
,
i.e.
there are dotted arrows as follows:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{0}\mathbin{\circ}(\delta^{1-k}\otimes X)}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{(I\otimes X)+_{X}Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}f}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{1-k}\otimes Y}
\textstyle{I\otimes Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

such that the two horizontal composites are identities.

First, by a standard diagram-chasing argument, giving the square on the right is equivalent to giving maps
\phi\colon I\otimes X\to X
,
g\colon Y\to X
,
\psi\colon I\otimes Y\to Y
such that the following diagrams commute:
