Theorem 7.8
.

The awfs
(\mathsf{C_{t}},\mathsf{F})
satisfies the functorial Frobenius condition.

First, we show that
(\mathsf{Cof}\times_{\cal{E}}\cal{S},\mathsf{Fib},\mathsf{Cof}\times_{\cal{E}}\cal{S})
satisfies the generalized functorial Frobenius condition.
This follows from
proposition

5.10
: by condition (S3) in the definition of
definition

6.1
and
proposition

5.4
,
(\mathsf{Cof},\cal{E}^{\to},\mathsf{Cof})
satisfies the generalized functorial Frobenius condition, hence also
(\mathsf{Cof},\mathsf{Fib},\mathsf{Cof})
by
proposition

5.3
; while
(\cal{S},\mathsf{Fib},\cal{S})
satisfies the generalized Frobenius condition by
lemma

7.7
.

The generalized functorial Frobenius condition for
(\mathsf{Cof}\times_{\cal{E}}\cal{S},\mathsf{Fib},\mathsf{Cof}\times_{\cal{E}}\cal{S})
implies the generalized functorial Frobenius condition for
(\cal{I}_{\otimes},\mathsf{Fib},\mathsf{TrivCof})
using functoriality of generalized functorial Frobenius in the form of
proposition

5.3
with
F
the functor in (
i
) of
proposition

7.5
,
G
the identity, and
H
the functor in (
ii
) of
proposition

7.5
.
This implies the functorial Frobenius condition for
(\mathsf{C_{t}},\mathsf{F})
by
theorem

5.9
.
∎

As special cases, we obtain the pushforward versions of the Frobenius and Beck-Chevalley condition for uniform fibrations.
First, pushforward lifts to slices of the category of uniform
\cal{I}
-fibrations.

Corollary 7.9
.

(i)

For every uniform fibration

p\colon X\to Y
, pushforward along
p
lifts to a functor

\textstyle{\mathsf{Fib}_{/X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{p_{*}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Fib}_{/Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cal{E}_{/X}^{\to}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{*}}
\textstyle{\cal{E}_{/Y}^{\to}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

(ii)

For every map of uniform
\cal{I}
-fibrations
(s,t)\colon p\to q
, where
p\colon X\to Y
and
q\colon U\to V
, the canonical natural transformation
\psi\colon t^{*}q_{*}\to p_{*}s^{*}
lifts to a natural transformation

\textstyle{\mathsf{Fib}_{/U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{q_{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{s^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\textstyle\Downarrow\hbox to0.0pt{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\psi}$\hss}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Fib}_{/V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{t^{*}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Fib}_{/X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{p_{*}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Fib}_{/Y}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

If
(s,t)\colon p\to q
forms a pullback square, then
\widetilde{\psi}
is a natural isomorphism.

The claim follows from
theorem

7.8
using
theorems

5.9
and
5.7
.
∎

Example 7.10
.

The application of
corollary

7.9
in
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
shows that pushforward along a uniform Kan fibration preserves uniform Kan fibrations.
Since exponentiation is a special case of pushforward, this result shows also that if
X
is a uniform Kan complex (defined in the evident way) then
Y^{X}
is again a uniform Kan complex, for every
Y
.
In fact, for this easier result, only the base needs to be assumed uniform Kan.
It follows by setting
A=_{\operatorname{def}}1
and
B=_{\operatorname{def}}0
from the general statement that the Leibniz exponential of a uniform Kan fibration
p\colon X\to A
with a cofibration
i\colon B\to Y
gives a uniform Kan fibration
\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(i,p)
.

As is well known, this can be seen as follows.
Let
k\in\{0\,,1\}
.
By
proposition

6.4
,
\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta}^{k},p)
is a uniform trivial Kan fibration.
Since uniform cofibrations are closed under Leibniz product with
i
, by adjointness
\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(i,\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta}^{k},p))=\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(\bar{\delta}^{k},\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(i,p))
is a uniform trivial Kan fibration.
Combining the cases
k=0
and
k=1
and using
proposition

6.4
in the reverse direction, it follows that
\operatorname{\widehat{exp}}(i,p)
is a uniform Kan fibration.

8.
Uniform fibrations in presheaf categories

The aim of this final section is to study in more detail the notion of a uniform fibration in the case when
\cal{E}
is a presheaf category.
Let us begin by fixing the setting in which we shall be working.
First, let
\mathbb{C}
be a small category and fix
\cal{E}=_{\operatorname{def}}\mathrm{Psh}(\mathbb{C})
.
We write
\mathrm{y}\colon\mathbb{C}\to\cal{E}
for the Yoneda embedding.
We assume that the functorial cylinder
I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
satisfies not only our standing assumptions of having contractions and connections and of possessing a right adjoint (see
section

1
), but also the following two conditions:

(C1)

I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
preserves pullback squares,

(C2)

the natural transformations
\delta^{k}\otimes(-)\colon\operatorname{Id}_{\cal{E}}\to I\otimes(-)
, for
k\in\{0\,,1\}
, are cartesian.

Second, we let
\cal{M}
be a full subcategory of
\cal{E}^{\to}_{\text{{cart}}}
satisfying the following assumptions:

(M1)

the elements of
\cal{M}
are monomorphisms,

(M2)

for every
X\in\cal{E}
, the unique map
\bot_{X}\colon 0\to X
is in
\cal{M}
,

(M3)

the elements of
\cal{M}
are closed under pullback,

(M4)

the elements of
\cal{M}
are closed under Leibniz product with the endpoint inclusions,

(M5)

the category
\cal{M}
has colimits and the inclusion
\cal{M}\to\cal{E}^{\to}_{\mathrm{cart}}
preserves them.
2
2

2

This assumption is not used to instantiate our general results, but only for
lemmas

8.7
and
8.9
.

The next result not only provides us with a wide class of examples of suitable awfs’s, but also shows that there are situations in which object-wise assertions, as in (M2)-(M4) above, can be strengthened to functoriality properties, as required to obtain a suitable weak awfs (
definition

6.1
).

Theorem 8.1
.

There exists a suitable awfs
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
on
\cal{E}
that is algebraically-free on
\cal{M}
,
i.e.
such that
\mathsf{TrivFib}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.92432pt\hskip 13.92432pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.92432pt\hskip 13.92432pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-11.93694pt\hskip 11.93694pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-11.93694pt\hskip 11.93694pt}}}}
.

Let
\cal{I}
be the full subcategory of
\cal{M}
spanned by maps with a representable presheaf as codomain.
Since
\cal{I}
is small, by Garner’s small object argument
[
16
]
it generates a cofibrantly generated awfs
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
with
\mathsf{TrivFib}={\mathsf{F_{t}}}\text{-}\mathsf{Map}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}}
.
Furthermore, we have
{\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.92432pt\hskip 13.92432pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.92432pt\hskip 13.92432pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-11.93694pt\hskip 11.93694pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-11.93694pt\hskip 11.93694pt}}}}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}}
.
This follows from
proposition

4.16
using that
\cal{M}
is a full subcategory of
\cal{E}^{\to}_{\text{{cart}}}
.
Indeed, for a map
f\colon X\to Y
in
\cal{E}
, to give a natural choice of fillers for all diagrams with an arbitrary element of
\cal{M}
on the left is the same as to give a natural choice of fillers for only those diagrams which are the form

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{y}(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y}

for
x\in\mathbb{C}
.

It remains to check that
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
satisfies conditions (S2)-(S4) in
definition

6.1
.
These follow from the corresponding object-wise assumptions (M2)-(M4) via standard diagram-chasing arguments, which we omit.
For condition (S4), the crucial property being used in that proof is that elements of
\cal{M}
, being monomorphisms in a topos, are adhesive maps
[
18
]
.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

Let us point out that the reduction to the lifting problems against maps in
\cal{M}
to those with a representable codomain in the proof of
theorem

8.1
exploits the good behaviour of the right orthogonality functor with respect to colimits as described in
proposition

4.16
, which is not available in the setting of ordinary wfs’s.

Example 8.3
.

Since assumptions (C1)-(C2) and (M1)-(M5) are fulfilled in both
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
, with
\cal{M}
the category of all monomorphisms and pullback squares (with (M5) a consequence of the existence of a subobject classifier),
theorem

8.1
establishes the existence of the awfs
(\mathsf{C_{t}},\mathsf{F})
of
example

6.6
.
The assumptions are also satisfied if instead in
\mathbf{CSet}
we choose for
\cal{M}
the category
\cal{M}^{\prime}
of
example

6.2
(with elements classified by the face lattice
\Phi
of
[
11
]
), establishing the existence of the awfs
(\mathsf{C_{t}}^{\prime},\mathsf{F}^{\prime})
of
example

6.6
.

Remark 8.4
.

We discuss how, in the case of
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
, the existence of the awfs’s
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
, having uniform trivial Kan fibrations as monad algebras, and
(\mathsf{C_{t}},\mathsf{F})
, having uniform Kan fibrations as monad algebras, and the Frobenius property for
(\mathsf{C_{t}},\mathsf{F})
can be proved constructively,
i.e.
without using the law of excluded middle or the axiom of choice.

We begin by observing that
theorem

8.1
can be proved constructively in
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
.
In order to see why this is the case, first note that in these examples every subobject of a representable is finitely presentable and that the functorial cylinder
I\otimes(-)\colon\cal{E}\to\cal{E}
preserves finitely presentable objects.
Since subobjects of representables are finitely presentable, the values of the inclusion
u\colon\cal{I}\to\cal{E}^{\to}
are finitely presentable objects of
\cal{E}^{\to}
.
An inspection of the proof of
[
16
, Theorem 4.4]
shows that this suffices to construct the algebraically-free awfs
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
on
u\colon\cal{I}\to\cal{E}^{\to}
, and in fact the sequence constructing the appropriate free monad converges after
\omega
steps.
Next, note that also
theorem

6.5
can be proved constructively for
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
, via a reasoning that is analogous to the one above, observing that also the values of
u_{\otimes}\colon\cal{I}_{\otimes}\to\cal{E}^{\to}
are finitely presentable by the assumption of the functorial cylinder and the fact that finitely presentable objects are closed under pushout.
Finally, the general proofs of
theorems

7.8
and
7.9
, establishing the functorial Frobenius condition for
(\mathsf{C_{t}},\mathsf{F})
and its pushforward analogue, are constructive.

We have therefore obtained a constructive proof that pushforward along a uniform Kan fibration preserves uniform Kan fibrations in
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
.
For
\mathbf{SSet}
, this result is a constructive counterpart of the fact that pushforward along a Kan fibration preserves Kan fibrations, which cannot be proved constructively without changes to the definition of Kan fibration
[
6
]
.

Finally, let us point out that if one adds the assumption that elements of
\cal{M}
are
decidable
monomorphisms, then the argument above carries over without relying on the Power Set axiom to establish the smallness of
\cal{I}
.

The rest of this section is devoted to giving a characterization of uniform trivial fibrations in terms of the partial map classifier, a result suggested to us by Thierry Coquand and André Joyal (see also
remark

8.5
below).
We then use this characterization to relate non-algebraic and algebraic notions of (trivial) fibration.

Remark 8.5
.

A related, but different, approach to generating an awfs
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
from a class of monomorphisms is taken in
[
7
]
.
Recall from
theorem

8.1
that
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
is cofibrantly generated by the full subcategory of
\cal{E}^{\to}_{\text{{cart}}}
spanned by monomorphisms, written
\cal{M}
below.
Instead, as done in
[
7
]
, one may assume that the chosen monomorphisms are closed under composition and view
\cal{M}
as a double category.
This then cofibrantly generates an awfs
(\mathsf{C}^{\prime},\mathsf{F_{t}}^{\prime})
in a double categorical sense.
The monad
\mathsf{F_{t}}^{\prime}
in the slice over
Y\in\cal{E}
is given by the
\cal{M}
-partial map classifier
P_{Y}
in the slice over
Y
defined before
theorem

8.8
below.
For us instead, as implies by
theorem

8.8
, the monad
\mathsf{F_{t}}
in the slice over
Y\in\cal{E}
is freely generated by
P_{Y}
seen as a pointed endofunctor.
It follows that the awfs’s
(\mathsf{C},\mathsf{F_{t}})
and
(\mathsf{C}^{\prime},\mathsf{F_{t}}^{\prime})
are different, but related by an isomorphism
{\mathsf{F_{t}}}\text{-}\mathsf{Map}\cong{\mathsf{F_{t}}^{\prime}}\text{-}\mathsf{map}
.
Since
\delta^{k}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}(-)
for
k\in\{0\,,1\}
do not lift to endofunctors on the double category of squares in
\cal{E}
, it is not clear how to construct a double categorical version of the generating uniform trivial cofibrations
\cal{I}_{\otimes}
to generate a corresponding awfs
(\mathsf{C_{t}}^{\prime},\mathsf{F}^{\prime})
.

We begin with a simple lemma.

Lemma 8.6
.

For all
X\in\cal{E}
,
\operatorname{id}_{X}\in\cal{M}
.

The claim follows by inspection of the following diagram:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}_{X}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Id}\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{0}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\bot_{X}}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Id}\otimes X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{0}\mathbin{\hat{\otimes}}\bot_{X}}
\textstyle{I\otimes X\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

By conditions (M2) and (M4), the right map is in
\cal{M}
.
By condition (C2), the square is a pullback.
By condition (M3), the left map
\operatorname{id}_{X}
is then in
\cal{M}
.
∎

Below, as usual, we write
\Omega
for the subobject classifier of the topos
\cal{E}
and
\top\colon 1\to\Omega
for its ‘true’ morphism.

Lemma 8.7
.

There exists a subobject
K\rightarrowtail\Omega
and a factorization

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{K\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

such that the map
1\to K
classifies maps in
\cal{M}
, in the sense that a map
i\colon A\to B
is in
\cal{M}
if and only if there exists a pullback

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{K\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

For
x\in\mathbb{C}
, define
K(x)
to be the set of subobjects of
\mathrm{y}(x)
that are elements of
\cal{M}
.
The factorisation of
\top\colon 1\to\Omega
through
K
follows since identities are contained in
\cal{M}
by
lemma

8.6
.

Any element
i\colon A\to B
of
\cal{M}
arises as base change of
1\to K
along a unique map
B\to K
, sending an element
b\colon\mathrm{y}(x)\to B
, for
x\in\mathbb{C}
, to the subobject of
\mathrm{y}(x)
given by the base change of
i\colon A\to B
along
b
, which is in
K(x)
since
i
is in
\cal{M}
.
For the converse, it suffices to show that
1\to K
is in
\cal{M}
, but this is equivalent to (M5).
∎

The axioms for the class
\cal{M}
in (M1)-(M5) can then be rewritten in equivalent form as properties of the classifier
K
, see
[
32
]
for details and
[
24
,
37
]
for related ideas in synthetic domain theory.
Observe that the factorization of
\top\colon 1\to\Omega
via
K
gives us a pullback diagram

14.76389pt\raise-36.48608pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.15279pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{t}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.53471pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.53471pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{K\times Y}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

for every
X\in\cal{E}_{/Y}
.

Theorem 8.8
.

Giving the structure of a uniform trivial fibration on a map
f\colon X\to Y
is equivalent to giving a diagonal filler for the diagram

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 16.56818pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.53471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.56818pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta_{X}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.90071pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.43542pt\raise-15.63889pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.43542pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-14.90071pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{Y}X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.42154pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.90071pt\raise-5.07709pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 39.42154pt\raise-31.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Y\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

i.e.
a retraction of
\eta_{X}
in
\cal{E}_{/Y}
.

Let
\cal{I}
be the full subcategory of
\cal{M}
spanned by maps with a representable presheaf as codomain and recall from the proof of
theorem

8.1
that we have
{\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-17.45413pt\hskip 17.45413pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.92432pt\hskip 13.92432pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-13.92432pt\hskip 13.92432pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{M}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-11.93694pt\hskip 11.93694pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-11.93694pt\hskip 11.93694pt}}}}={\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}}
.
Let
u\colon\cal{I}\to\cal{E}^{\to}
denote the inclusion.
Following the first step of Garner’s small object argument, a lift of a map
f\colon X\to Y
to an element of
{\mathchoice{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\displaystyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-10.89029pt\hskip 10.89029pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-9.051pt\hskip 9.051pt}}}{{\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\smash{\cal{I}}\vphantom{I}}$}^{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt\pitchfork}_{\scriptscriptstyle\hskip 0.0pt\hskip-8.19528pt\hskip 8.19528pt}}}}
is given by a diagonal filler in the canonical square
(\operatorname{Lan}_{u}u)(f)\to f
.
Unfolding the left Kan extension, we have

\displaystyle(\operatorname{Lan}_{u}u)(f)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{i\in\cal{I},u_{i}\to f}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{\sigma:\mathrm{y}C\to K\times Y,\ \cal{E}_{/Y}(t^{*}\sigma,X)}\sigma^{*}t

\displaystyle=\operatorname{colim}_{\sigma:\mathrm{y}C\to P_{Y}(X)}\sigma^{*}\eta_{X}

\displaystyle=\eta_{X}.

Here, the penultimate step uses the pullback square (
8.2
) and the last step uses the Yoneda lemma and preservation of colimits by pullback.
∎

Theorem 8.9
.

(i)

A map can be equipped with the structure of a uniform trivial fibration if and only if it is a trivial fibration,
i.e.
it has the right lifting property with respect to the maps in
\cal{M}
.

(ii)

A map can be equipped with the structure of a uniform fibration if and only if it is a fibration,
i.e.
it has the right lifting property with respect to the generating trivial cofibrations.

For part (i), since
1\to K
is in
\cal{M}
, as proved in
lemma

8.7
, then also
\eta
has components in
\cal{M}
by (
8.2
).
Part (ii) follows from part (i) and
proposition

6.4
.
∎

Example 8.10
.

theorem

8.9
applies to
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
, showing that a map can be equipped with the structure of a (trivial) Kan fibration if and only if it is a (trivial) Kan fibration in the usual sense.

Remark 8.11
.

Since
theorem

8.9
is proved constructively, it is natural to wonder whether it is possible to develop constructively our theory in the non-algebraic setting, at least for
\mathbf{SSet}
or
\mathbf{CSet}
.
The key obstacle to this is that, while the special case of the algebraic result in
theorem

8.1
admits a constructive proof in
\mathbf{SSet}
and
\mathbf{CSet}
, as we explained in
remark

8.4
, its non-algebraic counterpart does not seem to provable be constructively, even in the case when
\cal{M}
consists of all monomorphisms (
cf.
examples

2.2
and
8.2
).
And such a result is essential for the development since it provides the suitable wfs on which the definition of a fibrations is based.
